The screened denominator Eq. (
9.41
) is the central result of this work.

9.3.2
Screened Bloch-Siegert shift

Following the same calculation as above, the screened Bloch-Siegert shift,
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
, has the same form as the screened Stark shift (
9.40
), except for a substitution
\Delta_{\bf q}^{0}\to\Delta_{\bf q}^{0}+2{\omega}
in the expression of
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
therein. The resulting denominator
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}^{BS}
for the screened Bloch-Siegert shift reads

\begin{split}\Delta_{{\bf q},s}^{BS}&=\Delta_{\bf q}^{0}+2{\omega}-U_{11}\nu_{\bar{s}}+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{s^{\prime}}~~-~~\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf q}^{\prime\prime}}\langle\hat{n}_{{\bf q}^{\prime\prime},s}\rangle\frac{-\Delta_{\bf q}^{0}-2{\omega}+U_{11}\nu_{\bar{s}}-U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{s^{\prime}}}{-\Delta_{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}^{0}-2{\omega}+U_{11}\nu_{\bar{s}}-U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{s^{\prime}}}\\
&\approx\Delta_{\bf q}^{0}+2{\omega}-(U_{11}-U_{12})\nu_{\bar{s}}\end{split}

This derivation is equivalent to Eq. (
9.33
)
\sim
(
9.40
), and we only need to make the substitution in the Green operators therein.

9.3.3
Screened cavity-electron vertex and screened cavity-mediated interaction

We next study the term
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
, which appears from the expansion of Eq. (
2.18
) with the Dyson series (
9.2
). Again, we first consider a part of this term,
\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
, which reads,

\begin{split}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}_{(E_{\alpha}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}&\approx\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}\sum_{{\bf k},s,{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}^{*}g}{\sqrt{N}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}2s^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(\hat{g}\hat{U})^{n}\hat{g}\hat{b}_{{\bf k},s}^{{\dagger}}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&=\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}\sum_{{\bf k},s,{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}^{*}g}{\sqrt{N}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}2s^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{{\bf q}}\hat{b}_{{\bf q},s}^{{\dagger}}[\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}({\rm g}*{\rm f}^{s})^{n}*{\rm g}]_{{\bf q},{\bf k}}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&=\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}\sum_{{\bf q^{\prime}},{\bf q},s}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}^{*}g}{\sqrt{N}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}1s}\sum\limits_{\bf k}[({\rm g}^{-1}-{\rm f}^{s})^{-1}]_{{\bf q},{\bf k}}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&\approx\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}\sum_{{\bf q^{\prime}},{\bf q},s}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}^{*}g}{\sqrt{N}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}1s}(-\Delta_{{\bf q},s})^{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&=-\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}\sum_{{\bf k},{\bf q^{\prime}},s}\bigg(\frac{gg_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}^{*}}{\sqrt{N}\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\bigg)\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\end{split}

(9.42)

where in the first line we insert the definition of
\hat{H}_{-1}
and
\hat{H}_{c}
, expand
\hat{G}^{b}
over infinite orders of
\hat{U}
, and reduce the terms using
\hat{b}_{{\bf k},s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}=0
. Here we also discard the off-resonant part in
\hat{H}_{c}
, as we did in the last line of (
9.19
), so we restrict ourselves to the single-excitation subspace.
In the second line, we move
\hat{b}^{{\dagger}}
to the left, exactly as what we did in (
9.33
). In the third line, we use
\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}2s^{\prime}}\hat{b}_{{\bf q},s}^{{\dagger}}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}=\delta_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q}}\delta_{s^{\prime},s}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}1s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
which follows directly from (
9.1
). We use the matrix Taylor expansion to replace the infinite summation by the inverse of a single matrix. In the fourth line, we repeat the evaluation (
9.36
), and again make the mean-field decoupling (
9.31
) and (
9.32
), which results in the same renormalised denominator
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
in (
9.41
).

Comparing (
9.42
) with (
9.19
), we see that after including
\hat{U}
,
the term
\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
is still reduced to a scattering vertex between cavity photon and a lower-band electron, albeit the laser-bandgap detuning in its denominator is screened to Eq. (
9.41
).
Having derived the screened cavity-electron vertex, we come back to the term
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
,

\begin{split}&{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&\approx{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\bigg(\frac{g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}g^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}}\bigg)\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\times\hat{a}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{U})^{n}\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}~~\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{{\bf k},{\bf q},s}\bigg(\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}g}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\bigg)\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&={\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bf k},{\bf q},s\\
{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{|g|^{2}g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}\hat{U})^{n}\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&\approx{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bf k},{\bf q},s\\
{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{|g|^{2}g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&\approx-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bf k},{\bf q},s\\
{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{|g|^{2}g_{c,{\bf q^{\prime}}}g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{c}\Delta_{{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}.\end{split}

(9.43)

where in the first line we use the expression (
9.42
) for the screened vertex. In the second line we move
\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}
to the far-left using (
9.4
), and then we reduce this operator using
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{a}\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}={\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
which follows from the low-energy limit (
9.1
).
In the third line, we use
[\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})},\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}]\approx 0
to switch these two operators. As explained in Appendix
9.5
, this neglects the screening of the cavity-electron vertex, which is appropriate as long as
g_{c,{\bf q}}
remains non-zero only for extremely small
\bf q
.
In the fourth line, we use Eq. (
9.5
) to replace
\hat{G}^{b}
by a denominator related to the laser-cavity detuning
\Delta_{c}
. Again, comparing Eq. (
9.43
) with the unscreened interaction (
9.20
), the expansion term

{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
still results in the cavity-mediated interaction, albeit with renormalized interaction strength.

9.3.4
Screened non-RWA corrections to the cavity-mediated interaction

The leading screening effect on the non-RWA corrections to the cavity-mediated interaction (
9.21
) stems from the term
\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{0}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}
in (
2.18
), instead of the term
\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{G}^{0}_{({E_{\alpha}}-{\omega})}\hat{H}_{1}
which gives the Bloch-Siegert shift.
The expression of this screened interaction

{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{2,1}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{2,1}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}

is obtained by a substitution
\Delta_{\bf q}^{0}\to\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
in (
9.21
). Here the screened denominator
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
is the same as in (
9.41
).

9.3.5
Screened inter-manifold terms in the Floquet Hamiltonian

In the presence of on-site repulsion, in the Floquet Hamiltonian, the leading inter-manifold term
\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}
becomes screened. This term reads

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{2,0}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}_{(E_{\alpha}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}&=g^{2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{2,0}\sum_{{\bf q},{\bf q}^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}2s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}1s^{\prime}}\hat{G}^{b}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&\approx-\sum_{{\bf q},{\bf q}^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}}\frac{g^{2}}{\Delta_{{\bf q},s}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}2s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}1s^{\prime}}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\end{split}

(9.44)

Comparing (
9.44
) with the unscreened result (
9.22
), we see that the off-resonant effects are screened according to the renormalisation on the denominator (
9.41
).

9.4
Approximations on the commutator
[\hat{U},\hat{b}^{\dagger}]

9.4.1
Commutator terms

The commutation relation (
9.28
) of the main text is our central approximation in Chapter
4
. It discards all non-commuting terms other than the Hartree-/Fock-type terms.
We justify it in detail here: Using the definition of
\hat{U}=\hat{U}_{11}+\hat{U}_{12}+\hat{U}_{22}
in momentum space (
3.15
) and (
3.16
), we have

\begin{split}&\hat{U}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}=\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\hat{U}+\big[\hat{U}_{11},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]+\big[\hat{U}_{12},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]+\big[\hat{U}_{22},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]\\
&=\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\hat{U}-2\frac{U_{11}}{2N}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s}\\
&~~~~~~~~-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{\bf q^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s}+\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}-\hat{c}_{{\bf q},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},2,s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s}\\
&~~~~~~~~-2\frac{U_{22}}{2N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},2,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}-{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},2,\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\\
&=\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big(\hat{U}-\frac{U_{11}}{N}\sum\limits_{\bf k}\hat{n}_{{\bf k},1,\bar{s}}+\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k},s^{\prime}}\hat{n}_{{\bf k},1,s^{\prime}}\big)-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\big(\sum\limits_{\bf k}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\big)\hat{n}_{{\bf q},1,s}~~+~~...\end{split}

(9.45)

where in the last line we keep only three terms:

1) In
\big[\hat{U}_{11},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
, we only keep
{\bf q^{\prime}}=0
term

-\frac{U_{11}}{N}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}}=0}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s}~=~-\frac{U_{11}}{N}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}}}\hat{n}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}

corresponding to the intra-band Hartree term. The semi-classical justification for this treatment is as follows: When
{\bf q^{\prime}}\neq 0
, the expectation value
\sum_{\bf k^{\prime}}\langle\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}\rangle
represents the charge density wave of the lower-band electron with spin
\bar{s}
at wave-vector
\bf q^{\prime}
. However, this expectation value becomes much stronger at
{\bf q^{\prime}}=0
, because in this case the expectation value
\sum_{\bf k^{\prime}}\langle\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}\rangle
represents the total electron number in the lower-band with spin
\bar{s}
. Thus when the electron density is high, we can ignore all terms in
\big[\hat{U}_{11},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
except for the
{\bf q^{\prime}}=0
contribution.

2) In
\big[\hat{U}_{12},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
, we only keep two terms: the first term is the inter-band Hartree term where (as justified above) we take
{\bf q^{\prime}}=0
in the following summation,

\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}}=0,s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}~=~\frac{U_{12}}{N}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\hat{n}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}.

The second term is the inter-band Fock term, where we take
{\bf q^{\prime}}={\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}
in the following part of
\big[\hat{U}_{12},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]

\begin{split}&-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{\bf q^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s}+\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf q^{\prime}}={\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}\atop{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}=s}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}\\
&=-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{\bf q^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s}+\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}(1-\hat{n}_{{\bf q},1,s})\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,s}\\
&=-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{n}_{{\bf q},1,s}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,s}\\
&=-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\big(\sum\limits_{{\bf k}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k},1,s}\big)\hat{n}_{{\bf q},1,s}~+~\frac{U_{12}}{N}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\\
&\approx-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\big(\sum\limits_{{\bf k}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k},1,s}\big)\hat{n}_{{\bf q},1,s}~~\text{when the electron number}\gg 1\end{split}

In the last line we discard the term
\frac{U_{12}}{N}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}
, as it will be much smaller than the inter-band Hartree term
-\frac{U_{11}}{N}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\sum_{{\bf k^{\prime}}}\hat{n}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,\bar{s}}
(which has the same form), when the total electron number in the lower-band with spin
\bar{s}
is large. The semi-classical justification for only keeping the
{\bf q^{\prime}}={\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}\And s^{\prime}=s
part in the above summation is that, when ignoring the electron correlation in the lower-band, the expectation value will vanish
\langle\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\rangle=0
unless
{\bf q^{\prime}}={\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}\And s^{\prime}=s
. Thus, when the electron correlation effect in the lower-band is not strong, we can discard all terms other than
{\bf q^{\prime}}={\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}\And s^{\prime}=s
in the summation.

Besides, in
\big[\hat{U}_{12},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
, we completely discard the last term

\hat{c}_{{\bf q},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},2,s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s}

because this term vanishes in the low-energy limit where
\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},2,s^{\prime}}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}=0
, which is the only case we will encounter in the following evaluations.

3) For the same reason, the commutator
\big[\hat{U}_{22},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
can be ignored in the low-energy limit.

Altogether, in the last line of (
9.45
), we keep the intra-band Hartree term, inter-band Hartree term and the inter-band Fock term of the commutator
\big[\hat{U},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
. The approximation made in (
9.45
) is analogous to the random phase approximation in the equation-of-motion method, as elucidated in
[
193
]
, where the commutator terms are discarded if their expectation value (under the non-interacting ground state) is 0.

9.4.2
Naming of the Hartree & Fock commutator terms

We next explain why we call the terms in Eq. (
9.45
) “Hartree”/“Fock” terms, this naming convention resembles the “Direct”/“Exchange” commuting terms in Ref
[
194
]
: For example, in the commutator
\big[\hat{U}_{12},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
, the term

\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},2,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf q},1,s}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}},1,s^{\prime}}

(9.46)

can be diagrammatically represented by Fig. (
9.1
), where the band-index is not distinguished, and the incoming (outgoing) arrow represents the annihilation (creation) operator in (
9.46
).

Figure 9.1
:
diagrammatic illustration of the commutator term (
9.46
) in
\big[\hat{U}_{12},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
, where the different band-indices are not distinguished out.

In our approximation , we only keep two graphs where the incoming and outgoing momentum lines are contracted, as shown in fig(
9.2
).

Figure 9.2
:
The “Hartree” and “Fock” terms in the commutator
\big[\hat{U}_{12},\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}\big]
, these terms respectively corresponds to two possible ways to contract the momentum and spin, making the diagram closed.

Cutting one fermion line in these close diagrams respectively results in the Hartree/Fock-like self energy diagrams. In fig(
9.2
), we find the left (“Hartree”) diagram is the
{\bf q^{\prime}}=0
part of fig(
9.1
), and the right (“Fock”) diagram is the
{\bf k^{\prime}}={\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}},s^{\prime}=s
part of fig(
9.1
). This is equivalent to the approximation we make in the last line of (
9.45
), and thus we denote the terms we kept there as “Hartree”/“Fock” terms.

In Eq. (
9.35
),
{\rm f}_{F}^{s}
only contains the contribution from the inter-band Fock term, while
{\rm g}_{H}^{s}
contains the contribution from the intra- and inter-band Hartree terms. Consequently, these two quantities are respectively represented by “F” and “H”.

9.5
Screening of the cavity mode in Eq. (
9.43
)

9.5.1
Screening of the cavity-electron interaction vertex

In the third line of Eq. (
9.43
), we switch
\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}
and
\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}
, which ignores the screening effect on the cavity mode given by the electron on-site repulsion. To understand why this approximation is valid in the parameter regimes considered in this paper, we study the following commuting relation of the intra-band electron-hole creation operator

\begin{split}\hat{U}_{11}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}&=\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\hat{U}_{11}+\frac{U_{11}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf q^{\prime}}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1\bar{s}}(\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}+{\bf q^{\prime}}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}-\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q^{\prime}}1s})\\
&\approx\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\hat{U}_{11}+\frac{U_{11}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1\bar{s}}(\hat{n}_{{\bf k}1s}-\hat{n}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s})\\
&=\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1s}\bigg(\delta_{{\bf k^{\prime}},{\bf k}}\delta_{s^{\prime},s}\hat{U}_{11}+\delta_{s^{\prime},\bar{s}}\frac{U_{11}}{N}(\hat{n}_{{\bf k}1s}-\hat{n}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s})\bigg)\end{split}

(9.47)

where in the second line, to be consistent with the approximation (
9.28
), we only keep the contribution from
{\bf q^{\prime}}={\bf q}
(note that the contribution from
{\bf q^{\prime}}={\bf 0}
automatically vanishes). After this approximation, we see from the third line that, this commutation relation has a similar momentum-summation structure as Eq. (
9.28
), allowing the evaluation of

\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}\hat{U}_{11})^{n}\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}\sum\limits_{\bf k,\bf q}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}

in the second line of (
9.43
), by introducing another two matrices whose elements are operators, similar to (
9.34
). However, we can avoid this complex evaluation by noting that the spatial scale of the cavity mode is much larger than the lattice constant, so that
g_{c,{\bf q}}
remains non-zero only for extremely small
\bf q
, and thus

\begin{split}\sum\limits_{{\bf k},{\bf q}}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\bigg(\frac{U_{11}}{N}\sum\limits_{\bf k^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1\bar{s}}(\hat{n}_{{\bf k}1s}-\hat{n}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s})\bigg)&\approx\frac{U_{11}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},\bf q}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1\bar{s}}\sum\limits_{\bf k}(\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{n}_{{\bf k}1s}-\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k}-{\bf q},s}}\hat{n}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s})\\
&=\frac{U_{11}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf k^{\prime}},\bf q}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}-{\bf q}1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1\bar{s}}\times 0~~=~~0\end{split}

(9.48)

where in the first line we assume
\Delta_{{\bf k},s}\approx\Delta_{{\bf k}-{\bf q},s}
since
\bf q
can only be a tiny number when
g_{c,{\bf q}}\neq 0
. Combining the Hartree-Fock treatment (
9.47
) with (
9.48
), we directly have the following commuting relation

\begin{split}&\hat{U}_{11}\sum\limits_{\bf k,\bf q}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\approx\sum\limits_{\bf k,\bf q}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\hat{U}_{11}\end{split}

(9.49)

this commuting relation means that

\begin{split}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}\sum\limits_{\bf k,\bf q}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k}-{\bf q}1s}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}&\approx\sum\limits_{\bf k,\bf q}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k}-{\bf q}1s}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(\hat{g}_{(E_{\alpha}-\Delta_{c}-\epsilon_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1}+\epsilon_{{\bf k}1})}\hat{U})^{n}\hat{g}_{(E_{\alpha}-\Delta_{c}-\epsilon_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1}+\epsilon_{{\bf k}1})}\\
&\approx\sum\limits_{\bf k,\bf q}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k}-{\bf q}1s}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(\hat{g}_{(E_{\alpha}-\Delta_{c})}\hat{U})^{n}\hat{g}_{(E_{\alpha}-\Delta_{c})}\\
&=\sum\limits_{\bf k,\bf q}\frac{g_{c,{\bf q}}^{*}}{\Delta_{{\bf k},s}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k}-{\bf q}1s}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\hat{G}^{b}_{(E_{\alpha}-\Delta_{c})}\\
\end{split}

(9.50)

where in the first line, we apply the Dyson expansion to
\hat{G}^{b}
, and then, Eq.(
9.4
) and (
9.49
) together allow us to move
\hat{G}^{b}
rightward. In the second line we use
\Delta_{c}+\epsilon_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1}-\epsilon_{{\bf k}1}\approx\Delta_{c}
, which is again very accurate since
\bf q
can only be a tiny number when
g_{c,{\bf q}}\neq 0
, so that
\Delta_{c}\gg|\epsilon_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1}-\epsilon_{{\bf k}1}|
.
This is the reason why we can switch
\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}-\Delta_{c})}
and
\hat{c}_{{\bf k}-{\bf q}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}
in the third line of Eq. (
9.43
).

9.5.2
Screening by inter-band polarization bubbles

On top of the above screening of the cavity-electron vertex, throughout this work, we also ignore another type of expansion terms containing higher orders of
\hat{H}_{c}
, for example,

{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}

which also screens the cavity-mediated interaction (
9.43
). These screening terms can be understood as the inter-band polarization bubble screening, because the iterative application of the operator
\hat{H}_{c}\hat{G}^{b}
corresponds to the consecutive creation and annihilation of virtual inter-band excitons.

The cavity-mediated interaction cannot be strongly screened by these inter-band polarization bubbles, because this bubble comprises two electron-cavity vertices connected by an inter-band electron-hole propagator, giving rise to a factor
\sim|g_{c}|^{2}/(\Delta_{c}\Delta_{{\bf q},s}^{U})
. This coefficient is small under the off-resonating driving condition considered in this work, as shown in fig(
3.2
).

9.6
Low-temperature absorbance

The optical absorbance spectrum
\alpha(\omega)
for our model considered in Chapter
4
is defined as the imaginary part of the dipole-dipole correlation function in frequency domain, which, in the low-temperature limit, reads
[
262
,
204
]

\begin{split}\alpha(\omega)&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\text{Im}\langle G|\frac{1}{g^{*}}\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{\omega+E_{G}-\hat{H}_{0}+i\gamma}\frac{1}{g}\hat{H}_{-1}|G\rangle\\
&=-\frac{1}{|g|^{2}\pi}\text{Im}\langle G|\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{E_{G}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}_{(\omega+E_{G}+i\gamma)}^{0}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{E_{G}}|G\rangle.\end{split}

(9.51)

Here
\gamma
is a tiny positive number broadening the absorbance spectrum,
|G\rangle
is the ground state of the static Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
with eigenenergy
E_{G}
, and in the second line we use
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{E_{G}}|G\rangle=|G\rangle
which follows from the definition of the projector
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{E_{G}}=\delta(E_{G}-\hat{H}_{0})
. Compared with Eq. (
2.18
), the same Floquet low-energy Hamiltonian structure
\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}
appears in
\alpha(\omega)
in Eq. (
9.51
). This means that, to determine the absorbance at the driving frequency
\alpha({\omega})
, we just need to calculate the ground-state expectation value of (the RWA part of) our Floquet Hamiltonian.

When the driving frequency
{\omega}
becomes large enough so that
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
approaches 0 (at an arbitrary momentum
\bf q
with finite lower-band population) in Eq. (
4.14
), the screened optical Stark shift in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}
will diverge (because its strength is inversely proportional to
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
). This divergence of our Floquet Hamiltonian propagates into its ground-state expectation value,
\langle G|\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}|G\rangle
.
Thus, the absorbance spectrum
\alpha({\omega})
in Eq. (
9.51
) will also peak (or diverge if we take
\gamma\to 0
). An optical absorption peak at in-gap frequency indicates the exciton resonance.
Consequently, the excitonic resonance frequency
\omega_{\text{ex}}
is the smallest
{\omega}
for which the screened denominator
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
equals to 0.

9.7
Diagrammatic GRPA calculation

Below we compare our screened Floquet Hamiltonian (
4.11
) with an alternative method using the Matsubara formalism, where we construct Feynman diagrams in the laser-rotating frame. We move to the laser-rotating frame by applying the following unitary transformation to the original driven Hamiltonian (
3.5
),
H(t)\to U_{t}H(t)U_{t}^{{\dagger}}+i\hbar(\partial_{t}U_{t})U^{{\dagger}}_{t}
, and
|\psi\rangle_{t}\to U_{t}|\psi\rangle_{t}
, where

U_{t}=e^{i\omega_{L}t(\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}\hat{a}+\sum\limits_{\mathbf{q},s}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s})}.

(9.52)

Then, after discarding the counter-rotating terms (i.e applying the RWA in the laser-matter interaction), the dipolar Hamiltonian becomes static in the rotating frame,

\begin{split}\hat{H}_{\text{dip}}^{\text{rot}}&=\sum_{\mathbf{q},s}\big(\epsilon_{\mathbf{q},1}-\mu\big)\hat{n}_{\mathbf{q}1s}+\big(\epsilon_{\mathbf{q},2}-\mu-{\omega}\big)\hat{n}_{\mathbf{q}2s}+(\omega_{c}-{\omega})~\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}\hat{a}\\
&+\sum_{\mathbf{q},s}~(g+g_{c}\hat{a})\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}2s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{q}1s}+\text{ h.c. }\\
&+\hat{U}_{11}+\hat{U}_{22}+\hat{U}_{12}.\end{split}

(9.53)

Figure 9.3
:
The dressed single-particle propagators of two bands in the rotating frame. Note that the b=1 line will be used as the
hole
propagator in the lower-band. The Hartree contribution of
U_{22}
term is ignored in the b=2 line, for the reason discussed below Eq. (
9.28
).

Figure 9.4
:
The renormalised optical Stark shift, effective one-band electron-cavity vertex, and cavity-mediated interaction in the rotating frame. The wavy arrow denotes the cavity photon. The incoming and outgoing straight arrows denote the lower-band electron.

We next apply the usual Feynman diagrammatic approach to this Hamiltonian to study its effective low-energy response.
We will sum over a series of Feynman diagrams in the irreducible two-particle vertex, which in the static limit gives the equivalent result to Eq. (
4.14
). As shown in Fig.
9.3
, the electron and hole propagators in the Hamiltonian (
9.53
) are first dressed by the Hartree terms. The dressed propagators for the lower- and upper-band respectively read

\begin{split}\mathcal{G}_{1,{\bf q},s,iq_{n}}&=\frac{1}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}})}\\
\mathcal{G}_{2,{\bf q},s,iq_{n}}&=\frac{1}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q},2}-{\omega}-\mu+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}})}\end{split}

(9.54)

where the fermionic Matsubara Frequency is defined as
q_{n}=\frac{(2n+1)\pi}{\beta}
for all integer
n\in(-\infty,\infty)
. At low temperature we assume
\beta\to\infty
. The asymmetry between
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
arises from the fact that the upper-band is empty, so that
\nu_{2,s}=0
. The Fock self-energy disappears in these single particle propagators, because we only consider on-site electron-electron interactions.

The effective attraction between the screened electrons and holes, represented by the so-called GRPA polarisation diagrams
[
208
]
, are shown in Fig.
9.4
. All of these diagrams contain an opened polarization bubble
[
261
]
with an inter-band electron-hole t-matrix
[
263
]
, as shown in Fig.
9.5
. It reads

\begin{split}T&=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{U_{12}}{\beta N}\sum\limits_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}},iq_{n}}\frac{1}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}})}\frac{1}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}},2}-{\omega}-\mu+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}})}\bigg)^{n}\\
&=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}}\frac{-n_{F}(\epsilon_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}})+n_{F}(\epsilon_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}},2}-{\omega}-\mu)}{(\epsilon_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}})-(\epsilon_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}},2}-{\omega}-\mu+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}})}\bigg)^{n}\\
&=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(-\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}}\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{{\bf q}^{\prime\prime},1,s}\rangle}{-\Delta_{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}^{0}+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}-U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}}}\bigg)^{n}\\
&=\bigg(1+\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}}\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{{\bf q}^{\prime\prime},1,s}\rangle}{-\Delta_{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}^{0}+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}-U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}}}\bigg)^{-1}\\
\end{split}

(9.55)

where the Fermi distribution function is defined as
n_{F}(x)\equiv 1/(1+\exp(\beta x))
. In the third line, a geometric series similar to the infinite sum in Eq. (
9.36
) appears. Note that in the non-interacting model where
U_{12}\to 0
, this electron-hole t-matrix reduces to unity
T\to 1
, which is understood as a two-particle delta function (for both the upper-band electron line and the lower-band hole line). Meanwhile, note that when the exciton resonance requirement (
4.17
) is fulfilled, it diverges,
T\to\infty
.

Figure 9.5
:
The electron-hole t-matrix, which describes the attraction between the upper-band electron and the lower-band hole. The Matsubara frequency is not shown for simplicity.

The
t
-matrix allows us to calculate the renormalised optical Stark shift, the effective one-band electron-cavity vertex, and the cavity-mediated interaction (but not the Bloch-Siegert shift). The expressions are represented by the Feynman diagrams in Fig.
9.4
.
For example, the closed GRPA bubble for the optical Stark shift in Fig.
9.4
, contributes to a self-energy term for the lower-band electron, whose value at the 4-momentum
{\bf q},iq_{n}
evaluates to

\begin{split}\Sigma_{{\bf q},iq_{n}}~=~|g|^{2}\frac{1}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q},2}-{\omega}-\mu+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}})}T,\end{split}

(9.56)

and thus the laser-dressed propagator of the lower-band electron reads

\begin{split}&\mathcal{G}_{1,{\bf q},s,iq_{n}}^{\text{Fl}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{G}_{1,{\bf q},s,iq_{n}}^{-1}-\Sigma_{{\bf q},iq_{n}}}\\
&=\frac{1}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}+\frac{|g|^{2}T}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q},2}-{\omega}-\mu+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}})})}\end{split}

(9.57)

which has a pole at

\begin{split}iq_{n}&\to\epsilon_{{\bf q},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}+\frac{|g|^{2}T}{iq_{n}-(\epsilon_{{\bf q},2}-{\omega}-\mu+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}})}\\
&\approx\epsilon_{{\bf q},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}+\frac{|g|^{2}T}{(\epsilon_{{\bf q},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}})-(\epsilon_{{\bf q},2}-{\omega}-\mu+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}})}\\
&=\epsilon_{{\bf q},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}-\frac{|g|^{2}}{\big(\Delta_{\bf q}^{0}-U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}+U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}}\big)\bigg(1+\frac{U_{12}}{N}\sum\limits_{{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}}\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{{\bf q}^{\prime\prime},1,s}\rangle}{-\Delta_{\bf q^{\prime\prime}}^{0}+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}-U_{12}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}}\nu_{1,s^{\prime}}}\bigg)}\\
&=\epsilon_{{\bf q},1}-\mu+U_{11}\nu_{1,\bar{s}}-\frac{|g|^{2}}{\Delta_{{\bf q},s}}\end{split}

(9.58)

which exactly reveals the lower-band’s energy shift given by the screened optical Stark effect at spin-orbital
({\bf q},s)
in Eq. (
4.12
). Compared with the optical Stark shift in the non-interacting case, an excitonic enhancement with factor
T
is observed.
This shows that the renormalised denominator
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
, which appears in the renormalized optical Stark shift and the cavity-mediated interaction in the effective Floquet Hamiltonian (
4.11
), corresponds to the GRPA graphs in the retarded interaction formalism.

Figure 9.6
:
The additional Ladder diagrams for the self-energy in the lower-band, which are not included in our result. Merely including the analytically tractable diagrams in (a) will give non-physical result,
(\Delta_{{\bf q},s})^{-1}\propto T^{2}
.

One can include more ladder diagrams in the self-energy calculation, e.g. as shown in Fig.
9.6
(a), however, this would end up in a
T^{2}
enhancement on the optical Stark shift
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
showing several nonphysical features: this enhancement is not consistent with the exact result in the flat-bandgap limit (see the right 3 panels in Fig.
4.5
), and also, it restricts the sign of the detuning
\Delta_{{\bf q},s}
to be non-negative even when
{\omega}>\omega_{ex}
. As discussed in Ref.
[
264
]
, in order to include all possible ladder diagrams, one must simultaneously include other diagrams, e.g. the “line-crossing” graphs in Fig.
9.6
(b) and the “excitonic tadpole” graphs in Fig.
9.6
(c), which could cancel the nonphysical effects, but are analytically uneasy to tract. In our Green operator approach, the
T^{2}
enhancement can be reproduced by a consecutive mean-field decoupling on Eq. (
9.37
), but before including these additional terms, we must first retain more terms in the commutator relation Eq. (
9.45
), so as to keep the resulting Floquet Hamiltonian valid in the flat-bandgap limit. This improvement is left for future study.

9.8
The self-consistent derivation of
|g|^{4}
order Floquet Hamiltonian

9.8.1
The spurious Floquet-induced interaction in the in
|g|^{4}
order Floquet Hamiltonian

All of the driving-induced effects studied in Chapter
4
are to the second order of driving strength
|g|^{2}
within the approximation (
2.18
). Next we go beyond (
2.18
) to analyse the leading effect in the fourth order
|g|^{4}
, as these will be of similar size as the cavity-mediated interaction described above. According to Eq. (
2.17
), this leading term comes from the expansion
\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{-1}
, which reads (we assume the absence of electron interaction
\hat{U}=0
)

\begin{split}&{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}+2{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{g}_{({E_{\alpha}}+{\omega})}\hat{H}_{-1}{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}\\
&\approx\sum_{{\bf k},s,{\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime}}\frac{-|g|^{4}}{(\Delta_{\bf k}^{0})^{2}\Delta_{\bf k^{\prime}}^{0}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k^{\prime}}1s^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{{\bf k}1s}\end{split}

(9.59)

This expression holds only after excluding the case when
({\bf k},s)=({\bf k^{\prime}},s^{\prime})
in the summation, for the same reason explained below Eq. (
9.21
). In the derivation of Eq. (
9.59
), we use (
9.4
) to move all three
\hat{g}
to the right, and then use (
9.5
) to reduce them into denominators. In the virtual process described in Eq. (
9.59
), two upper-band electrons are sequentially excited by two
\hat{H}_{-1}
, and then annihilated by two
\hat{H}_{1}
. The resulting Floquet-induced interaction (
9.59
) has the same form as the interaction (
9.24
), but a much larger coefficient. Below we explain why Eq. (
9.59
) is a spurious Floquet-induced interaction, which hinders the application of the Gaussian elimination method in understanding the higher order driving effects in Chapter
4
.

First, note that the interaction term (
9.59
) is not a unique result of our Floquet method: In the alternative rotating-frame RWA method in appendix
9.7
, this leading
|g|^{4}
term also appears, as long as a projector-based perturbation technique is used to derive the low-energy effective Hamiltonian. Next, we explain the appearance of (
9.59
) in our Floquet formalism.

Terms like (
9.59
) and (
9.24
) are interactions mediated by Floquet “photons”. These interactions still appear in the Floquet Hamiltonian, even though the undriven many-body system is non-interacting. This strange behaviour is thoroughly explained in Ref.
[
129
]
: The interaction (
9.59
) generates electron correlations in the eigenstate
|\alpha,0\rangle
of our Floquet Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}
. However, this correlation in
|\alpha,0\rangle
arises merely because we map the many-body dynamics onto the 0-th Floquet sector. If we re-construct all
|\alpha,n\rangle
from
|\alpha,0\rangle
according to Eq. (
2.15
), we will find that the electrons in the
\alpha
-th Floquet state
|\alpha\rangle_{(t)}
described in (
2.3
) are again not correlated. In other words, in a driven many-body non-interacting system, it is the interaction (
9.59
) in the 0-th Floquet sector that prevents our Floquet method from predicting nonphysical electron-correlations in the real-time evolution.

If we treat the Sambe space eigenvalue problem (
2.15
) as a gauge theory with gauge freedom (
2.12
), whose gauge fixing process is described by (
2.13
), then the generated interaction (
9.59
) plays a similar role as the gauge ghost
[
265
]
: a gauge ghost cannot be physically observed, but it cancels the nonphysical effects arising from the gauge fixing process.

In addition to (
9.59
), there is another leading term in
|g|^{4}
order, which appears as we take into account the self-consistent change of
{E_{\alpha}}
in Eq. (
2.18
), as shown below. Thus in total there are two leading terms in
|g|^{4}
order, and their divergent behaviour cancel with each other.

9.8.2
Curing the issue within the Sambe space Gaussian elimination formalism remains challenging

Beyond the high-frequency limit, the shift of the eigenvalue
{E_{\alpha}}
must also be self-consistently tracked & recorded when deriving the Floquet Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}
. We start from the self-consistent eigenvalue problem (
2.16
) where
\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}
takes the form (
2.17
). In a self-consistent order-expansion of the eigenvalue problem (
2.16
) over
|g|
, we have

\begin{split}\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}&=\hat{H}_{0}+|g|^{2}\hat{H}^{(2)}+|g|^{4}\hat{H}^{(4)}+O[|g|^{6}]\\
{E_{\alpha}}&=E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}+|g|^{4}E_{\alpha}^{(4)}+O[|g|^{6}]\\
|\alpha,0\rangle&=|\alpha,0\rangle^{(0)}+|g|^{2}|\alpha,0\rangle^{(2)}+|g|^{4}|\alpha,0\rangle^{(4)}+O[|g|^{6}]\end{split}

(9.60)

where
{E_{\alpha}}
,
E_{\alpha}^{(0)}
and
|g|
have the dimension of energy, but
E_{\alpha}^{(2)}
has the dimension of one-over-energy. The reason why
|g|^{1}
order disappear in this expansion (
9.60
) is that, in the expression of
\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}
in (
2.17
), the driving operator
\hat{H}_{-1}\sim|g|
will always come in pair with its Hermitian conjugate
\hat{H}_{1}\sim|g|
, thus
\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}
only contains perturbations to the even orders of
|g|
. In future work, if we work in Coulomb gauge and take into account the diamagnetic coupling, the
e^{\pm i2{\omega}t}
driving frequency will appear in (
2.14
), which will make the structure of (
9.60
) more complex.

Expanding
\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}
to the
|g|^{4}
order, we have

\begin{split}\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}&=\hat{H}_{0}~+~\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{{E_{\alpha}}-\hat{H}_{0}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{{E_{\alpha}}-\hat{H}_{0}+2{\omega}-\ldots}\hat{H}_{-1}}\hat{H}_{-1}\\
&~~~~~~~~~+~\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{{E_{\alpha}}-\hat{H}_{0}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{{E_{\alpha}}-\hat{H}_{0}-2{\omega}-\ldots}\hat{H}_{1}}\hat{H}_{1}\\
&\approx\hat{H}_{0}+\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\\
&~~~~~~~~+\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+2{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\\
&~~~~~~~~+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-2{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\\
&\approx\hat{H}_{0}+\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}+\hat{H}_{1}\frac{-|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}}{\big(E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}\big)^{2}}\hat{H}_{-1}\\
&~~~~~~~~+\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+2{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\\
&~~~~~~~~+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{-|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}}{\big(E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}\big)^{2}}\hat{H}_{1}\\
&~~~~~~~~+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-2{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\\
\end{split}

(9.61)

where in the first line we insert the formula (
2.17
) for
\hat{H}^{\text{eff}}_{({E_{\alpha}})}
. In the second line we expand to the
|g|^{4}
order, and then we insert the expansion of
{E_{\alpha}}
in (
9.60
). In the third line we use Taylor expansion to move
E_{\alpha}^{(2)}
outside the denominator. Comparing (
9.61
) with (
9.60
), we identify
\hat{H}^{(2)}
and
\hat{H}^{(4)}
as

\begin{split}|g|^{2}\hat{H}^{(2)}&=\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\end{split}

(9.62)

\begin{split}|g|^{4}\hat{H}^{(4)}&=\hat{H}_{1}\frac{-|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}}{\big(E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}\big)^{2}}\hat{H}_{-1}+\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+2{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\\
&+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{-|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}}{\big(E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}\big)^{2}}\hat{H}_{1}+\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{-1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-2{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\frac{1}{E_{\alpha}^{(0)}-{\omega}-\hat{H}_{0}}\hat{H}_{1}\\
\end{split}

(9.63)

We see that,
\hat{H}^{(2)}
is nothing but our lowest-order Floquet Hamiltonian in (
2.18
). In
\hat{H}^{(4)}
, the term
\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{-1}
gives rise to the spurious Floquet-induced interaction in (
9.59
).

However, in
\hat{H}^{(4)}
, the term
-E_{\alpha}^{(2)}\hat{H}_{1}\big(\hat{G}^{0}\big)^{2}\hat{H}_{-1}
cannot be derived by our projector-based technique developed in the main text. This is because the energy filter
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
introduced in Section
4.2.1
cannot capture the self-consistent change of
{E_{\alpha}}
in the eigenvalue problem (
2.16
). Consequently, the current method based on
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
cannot capture any
|g|^{4}
term which contains the self-consistent energy shift
E_{\alpha}^{(2)}
. In future works, this drawback could possibly be fixed by choosing a more sophisticated many-body projector than our energy-filter
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
: For example in Ref.
[
192
]
, the many-body projectors used therein also undergo a self-consistent shift as the interaction strength is turned on.

The procedure of deriving
\hat{H}^{(4)}
is: First, use the 0-th order of the self-consistent eigenvalue problem (
2.16
)

\hat{H}_{0}|\alpha,0\rangle^{(0)}=E_{\alpha}^{(0)}|\alpha,0\rangle^{(0)}

to decide the 0-th order eigenvalue
E_{\alpha}^{(0)}
. Then, use
E_{\alpha}^{(0)}
to construct
\hat{H}^{(2)}
via (
9.62
). Then, use the 2-nd order of the eigenvalue problem (
2.16
)

\big(\hat{H}_{0}+|g|^{2}\hat{H}^{(2)}\big)\big(|\alpha,0\rangle^{(0)}+|g|^{2}|\alpha,0\rangle^{(2)}\big)=\big(E_{\alpha}^{(0)}+|g|^{2}E_{\alpha}^{(2)}\big)\big(|\alpha,0\rangle^{(0)}+|g|^{2}|\alpha,0\rangle^{(2)}\big)

to decide the self-consistent energy shift
E_{\alpha}^{(2)}
. At last, construct
\hat{H}^{(4)}
by inserting
E_{\alpha}^{(2)}
into (
9.63
). When
\hat{H}_{0}
describes a many-body system, the exact diagonalization of the above two eigenvalue problems becomes impossible. Thus we need to introduce a many-body projector, which not only circumvents the need of exact diagonalization (this circumvention is achieved by
{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{{E_{\alpha}}}}
in the main text), but also undergoes self-consistent shift. We leave this projector-based self-consistent derivation of
\hat{H}^{(4)}
for future works, and stick to the
|g|^{2}
effects in which no “gauge ghost effect” will be encountered.

At last we note that, for a many-body system with
\mathcal{N}
electrons in the lower-band, the “Floquet-induced interaction” term (
9.59
) in
\hat{H}^{(4)}
, at mean-field level, is
\frac{\mathcal{N}|g|^{2}}{(\Delta_{2})^{2}}
-times stronger than the optical Stark shift (
9.17
) in
\hat{H}^{(2)}
. In other words, the term
\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{-1}\hat{G}^{0}\hat{H}_{-1}
in
\hat{H}^{(4)}
shows a divergence (proportional to the particle-number
\mathcal{N}
). Meanwhile, the other term
-E_{\alpha}^{(2)}\hat{H}_{1}\big(\hat{G}^{0}\big)^{2}\hat{H}_{-1}
in
\hat{H}^{(4)}
also shows a divergence to the same order of particle-number, because
E_{\alpha}^{(2)}\propto\mathcal{N}
is the shift of the many-body eigen-energy. Future works should look into the cancellation between these two divergences, in analogy to the similar cancellations in equilibrium quantum many-body systems
[
266
,
267
]
.

It remains challenging to cure these self-consistency-related issues within the Gaussian elimination Floquet method developed in Chapter
4
. However, these issues are completely circumvented by the upgraded Floquet theory developed in Chapter
5
.

Chapter 10
Appendix for FSWT

10.1
The formal solution to the Sylvester equation

Here, we formally solve the Sylvester equations in our FSWT, based on the following mathematical relation (see Theorem 9.2 in Ref.
[
219
]
): For 3 arbitrary operators
\hat{a}
,
\hat{b}
and
\hat{c}
, where each eigenvalue of
\hat{a}
has positive real part, and each eigenvalue of
\hat{b}
is purely imaginary, the solution to the Sylvester equation
\hat{a}\hat{f}-\hat{f}\hat{b}=\hat{c}
is given by

\begin{split}\hat{f}&=-e^{-t\hat{a}}\hat{f}e^{t\hat{b}}\big|_{t=0}^{\infty}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~\partial_{t}\big(e^{-t\hat{a}}\hat{f}e^{t\hat{b}}\big)\\
&=-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~\partial_{t}\big(e^{-t\hat{a}}\big)\hat{f}e^{t\hat{b}}+e^{-t\hat{a}}\hat{f}\partial_{t}\big(e^{t\hat{b}}\big)\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~e^{-t\hat{a}}~\hat{a}\hat{f}~e^{t\hat{b}}-e^{-t\hat{a}}~\hat{f}\hat{b}~e^{t\hat{b}}\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~e^{-t\hat{a}}~\hat{c}~e^{t\hat{b}}\end{split}

(10.1)

If we take
\hat{a}=-i(\hat{H}^{(0)}+j{\omega})+0^{+}
,
\hat{b}=-i\hat{H}^{(0)}
and
\hat{c}=-i\hat{H}^{(1)}_{j}
, we can identify the lowest-order Sylvester equation (
5.12
) with this form, and thus we can directly write down its solution
\hat{f}^{(1)}_{j}
using Eq. (
10.1
):
\forall j\neq 0
,

\hat{f}_{j}^{(1)}=-i\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~e^{ij{\omega}t}~e^{-0^{+}t}~e^{i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}\hat{H}_{j}^{(1)}e^{-i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}

(10.2)

Eq. (
10.1
) further provides the formal solution to the Sylvester equation at higher orders. For example, to solve the second-order equation (
5.15
), we take
\hat{a}=-i(\hat{H}^{(0)}+j{\omega})+0^{+}
,
\hat{b}=-i\hat{H}^{(0)}
and
\hat{c}=-i\hat{S}^{(2)}_{j}
where

\hat{S}^{(2)}_{j}=\hat{H}^{(2)}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}\neq 0}[\hat{f}_{j^{\prime}}^{(1)},\hat{H}_{j-j^{\prime}}^{(1)}]+\frac{1}{2}[\hat{f}_{j}^{(1)},\hat{H}_{0}^{(1)}]

denotes the source term in the Sylvester equation (
5.15
). Eq. (
10.1
) then gives,
\forall j\neq 0
,

\hat{f}^{(2)}_{j}=-i\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~e^{ij{\omega}t}~e^{-0^{+}t}~e^{i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}\hat{S}_{j}^{(2)}e^{-i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}

(10.3)

In general, once the
n
th order Sylvester equation is constructed from which source term
\hat{S}_{j}^{(n)}
is identified, the formal solution is directly given by,
\forall j\neq 0
,

\hat{f}^{(n)}_{j}=-i\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~e^{ij{\omega}t}~e^{-0^{+}t}~e^{i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}\hat{S}_{j}^{(n)}e^{-i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}

(10.4)

which always takes the form of a frequency-domain (i.e. Laplace-transformed) Heisenberg operator under the undriven Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{(0)}
.

10.1.1
Link to the retarded Green function

The above formal solution links
\hat{f}^{(n)}_{j}
to the retarded Green function of the undriven system.
To show this, we consider the correlator
\langle[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{A}]\rangle
where
\hat{A}
is an observable and the expectation value is taken over the thermal/ground state of the undriven system
\hat{H}^{(0)}
. Then according to Eq. (
10.2
) we have

\langle[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{A}]\rangle=-i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt~e^{i({\omega}+i0^{+})t}~\theta_{t}\langle[\big(\hat{H}_{1}^{(1)}\big)^{H}_{t},\hat{A}]\rangle

(10.5)

where
\theta_{t}
is the step function at
t=0
,
\big(\hat{H}_{1}^{(1)}\big)^{H}_{t}=e^{i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}\hat{H}_{1}^{(1)}e^{-i\hat{H}^{(0)}t}
represents the Heisenberg operator of
\hat{H}_{1}^{(1)}
.
Hence,
\langle[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{A}]\rangle
is a frequency-domain retarded Green function of the undriven system
\hat{H}^{(0)}
.
This direct link between
\hat{f}^{(n)}_{j}
and the retarded Green function suggests that we can obtain the linear and non-linear responses of the system

1
1

1

For example, in a monochromatically driven system described by
\hat{H}_{t}=\hat{H}^{(0)}+\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}e^{-i\omega t}+\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}e^{i\omega t}
, by expanding the expectation value
\langle\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{t,t_{0}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{A}\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{t,t_{0}}\rangle
in orders of
g
and picking out terms oscillating at
e^{ij{\omega}t}
where
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we find that the
\mathcal{O}(g)
linear response of an observable
\hat{A}
is given by
\delta^{(1)}\langle\hat{A}\rangle=\langle[\hat{F}^{(1)}_{t},\hat{A}]\rangle
, and the
\mathcal{O}(g^{2})
response is given by
\delta^{(2)}\langle\hat{A}\rangle=\langle[\hat{F}^{(2)}_{t},\hat{A}]\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle[\hat{F}^{(1)}_{t},[\hat{F}^{(1)}_{t},\hat{A}]]\rangle
. This means we can directly compute these responses when
\hat{f}^{(n)}_{j}
is found.

from the solutions of the Sylvester equations.

10.1.2
Obtaining the spectral function by solving the Sylvester equation

In equilibrium-state many-body systems, quantities such as the spectral function can also be obtained by solving the Sylvester equations. According to Eq. (
10.1
), the Laplace-transformed Heisenberg operator

\hat{f}_{\omega}=-i\int_{0}^{\infty}dt~e^{i{\omega}t}~e^{-0^{+}t}~e^{i\hat{H}t}\hat{W}e^{-i\hat{H}t}

(10.6)

is the formal solution to the following Sylvester equation

\hat{W}+[\hat{f}_{\omega},\hat{H}]-({\omega}+i0^{+})\hat{f}_{\omega}=0.

(10.7)

The frequency-domain retarded Green function
G^{R}
can be directly obtained from
\hat{f}_{\omega}
, such that

2
2

2

More explicitly,
G^{R}=\langle[\hat{f}_{\omega},\hat{W}^{{\dagger}}]_{\pm}\rangle
where
\hat{W}=\hat{\psi}_{{\bf k},\sigma}
is the annihilation operator, and
\pm
represents the anti-commutator for Fermions and the commutator for Bosons, respectively.

G^{R}\sim\langle\hat{f}_{\omega}\hat{W}^{{\dagger}}\rangle
, thus the spectral function is given by
A\equiv-\frac{1}{\pi}\text{Im}[G^{R}]\sim\text{Im}[\langle\hat{f}_{\omega}\hat{W}^{{\dagger}}\rangle]
. We note that the imaginary part
i0^{+}
enters in Eq. (
10.7
) as we take
\hat{a}=-i(\hat{H}+{\omega})+0^{+}
in Eq. (
10.1
). Keeping track of this
i0^{+}
in Eq. (
10.7
) is necessary to obtain the spectral function
A
, but it causes no difficulty in solving the Sylvester equation: All the methods developed for solving the Sylvester equation in this thesis allows
{\omega}
to be complex.

10.2
The Sylvester Equation of the driven Hubbard chain

10.2.1
The
\mathcal{O}(g)
lowest order

Here we solve the lowest-order Sylvester equation (
6.6
) for the driven
L
-site Hubbard chain described by Eqs. (
6.2
) and (
6.5
), based on the approach used for the driven Hubbard dimer in Section
6.1.1
. We expand the solution
\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1}
in orders of hopping
J
, i.e.
\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{y}_{n}
where
\hat{y}_{n}\sim J^{n}
, and then Eq. (
6.6
) is decoupled into the set of Eqs. (
6.8
).

Due to the commutation relation
[\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{U}]=0
, the solution to Eq. (
6.8a
) is exactly given by

\hat{y}_{0}=\frac{\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}}{{\omega}}=\frac{g}{{\omega}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{L}j~\hat{n}_{j,s}

(10.8)

Then, to solve Eq. (
6.8b
) for
\hat{y}_{1}
, we need to calculate the commutator

[\hat{y}_{0},\hat{h}]=\frac{Jg}{{\omega}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{L}\left(\delta_{i-j,1}-\delta_{j-i,1}\right)\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}

(10.9)

which acts as the
source
of the
J^{1}
-order in Eq. (
6.8b
). Since the Sylvester equation is linear,
we only need to solve the following part (which sums to the final result for
\hat{y}_{1}
),

\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}+[\hat{x}_{1},\hat{U}]-{\omega}\hat{x}_{1}=0.

(10.10)

Since
\hat{U}
only contains local operators, the solution
\hat{x}_{1}
can only contain the degree of freedom of sites
i
and
j
, and thus we need to solve (for
i\neq j
)

\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}+[\hat{x}_{1},U\hat{n}_{i,\uparrow}\hat{n}_{i,\downarrow}+U\hat{n}_{j,\uparrow}\hat{n}_{j,\downarrow}]-{\omega}\hat{x}_{1}=0

(10.11)

which is no longer a many-body problem. According to the symmetry argument in Section
6.1.1
, the solution
\hat{x}_{1}
can only take the following form

\hat{x}_{1}=\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}\frac{1}{{\omega}}(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})

(10.12)

whose parameters can be determined by inserting Eq. (
10.12
) into Eq. (
10.11
) and then requiring the prefactors of each operator in Eq. (
10.11
) to vanish. The result is given in Eq. (
10.41
).
From this result Eq. (
10.12
), we know that the Sylvester equation Eq. (
6.8b
) with source term Eq. (
10.9
) has the solution

\begin{split}\hat{y}_{1}&=\frac{Jg}{{\omega}^{2}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{L}\left(\delta_{i-j,1}-\delta_{j-i,1}\right)\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~\times(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})\end{split}

(10.13)

Collecting
\hat{y}_{0}
and
\hat{y}_{1}
gives the solution Eq. (
6.11
) shown in the main part.
Next we further outline how to solve Eq. (
6.8c
) for
\hat{y}_{2}\sim J^{2}
. Its source term reads

\begin{split}[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{h}]&=\frac{2J^{2}g}{{\omega}^{2}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{L-1}\bigg\{~(\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})(\hat{c}_{j\uparrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\downarrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1\uparrow}\hat{c}_{j+1\downarrow}-\hat{c}_{j+1\uparrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1\downarrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\uparrow}\hat{c}_{j\downarrow})~~-\hat{n}_{j}+\hat{n}_{j+1}\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+(\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})\big(~\hat{n}_{j+1\uparrow}\hat{n}_{j+1\downarrow}(1-\hat{n}_{j})~-~\hat{n}_{j\uparrow}\hat{n}_{j\downarrow}(1-\hat{n}_{j+1})~\big)\bigg\}\\
&+\frac{J^{2}g}{{\omega}^{2}}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{L-1}\sum\limits_{s}\bigg\{~\hat{c}_{j-1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}\big((\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}-\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}}+(\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}(\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}-\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}})\big)\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1s}\big((\gamma^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime})\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}-\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}}+(\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}(\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}-\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}})\big)\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\hat{c}_{js}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}~\big((\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})+(\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})(\hat{n}_{j-1s}-\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\big)\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{js}~\big((\gamma^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime})+(\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})(\hat{n}_{j-1s}-\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\big)\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+(\hat{c}_{js}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{js})~(\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})(\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}-\hat{n}_{j-1s})~\bigg\}\end{split}

(10.14)

where we use
\delta^{\prime}=-\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}
and
\hat{n}_{j}=\sum_{s}\hat{n}_{j,s}
to simplify the result.
In the non-interacting limit
U\to 0
(where
\beta^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}=\delta^{\prime}=0
), we find the commutator
[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{h}]
reduces completely to boundary effects, which only contain the operators at the boundary sites
j=1,L
.
However, for
U\neq 0
,
[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{h}]
will become non-vanishing in the bulk. Since the Sylvester equation (
6.8c
) is linear, we can find the solution for each individual term in this
[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{h}]
, and then sum them up to get
\hat{y}_{2}
, just as what we did above to find
\hat{y}_{1}
. This means that to find
\hat{y}_{2}
, we need to solve 3-site Sylvester equations, such as

\begin{split}\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{k,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{i,s}+[\hat{x}_{2},\hat{U}]-{\omega}\hat{x}_{2}&=0,\\
\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{k,\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{i,s}+[\hat{x}_{3},\hat{U}]-{\omega}\hat{x}_{3}&=0,\end{split}

(10.15)

which is similar to Eq. (
10.10
). According to the same symmetry argument, the solution to Eq. (
10.15
) takes the form

\begin{split}\hat{x}_{2}&=\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{k,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{i,s}\frac{1}{{\omega}}(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{k,s}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{k,s}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})\\
\hat{x}_{3}&=\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j,\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{k,\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{i,s}\frac{1}{{\omega}}\big(\frac{{\omega}}{{\omega}+U}-\beta^{\prime}(\hat{n}_{k,s}+\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})-\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{k,s}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}\big)\end{split}

(10.16)

where the coefficients are decided by Eq. (
10.15
). The solution of
\hat{y}_{2}
is then constructed from
\hat{x}_{2}
, according to the source term
[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{h}]
. In the final solution of
\hat{y}_{2}
, each term will contain a common factor
J^{2}g/{\omega}^{3}
, together with a term-specific factor that makes
\hat{y}_{2}
accurate at arbitrary
U/J
ratio. In our driven Hubbard chain,
\hat{y}_{2}
is found to be

\begin{split}\hat{y}_{2}&=\frac{2J^{2}g}{{\omega}^{3}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{L-1}\bigg\{~(\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})(\hat{c}_{j\uparrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\downarrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1\uparrow}\hat{c}_{j+1\downarrow}-\hat{c}_{j+1\uparrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1\downarrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\uparrow}\hat{c}_{j\downarrow})~~-\hat{n}_{j}+\hat{n}_{j+1}\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+(\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime})\big(~\hat{n}_{j+1\uparrow}\hat{n}_{j+1\downarrow}(1-\hat{n}_{j})~-~\hat{n}_{j\uparrow}\hat{n}_{j\downarrow}(1-\hat{n}_{j+1})~\big)\bigg\}\\
&+\frac{J^{2}g}{{\omega}^{3}}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{L-1}\sum\limits_{s}\\
&\bigg\{~\hat{c}_{j-1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}\big((\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}-\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}}-\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}(\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}-\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}})\big)\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\times(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\\
&+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1s}\big((\gamma^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime})\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}-\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}}-\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}(\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}-\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}})\big)\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\times(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{j-1\bar{s}})\\
&+\hat{c}_{js}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}~\big((\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})-\delta^{\prime}(\hat{n}_{j-1s}-\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\big)(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1s}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1s}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\\
&+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{js}~\big((\gamma^{\prime}-\beta^{\prime})-\delta^{\prime}(\hat{n}_{j-1s}-\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\big)(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1s}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{j-1s})\\
&+\hat{c}_{js}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}~\delta^{\prime}(\hat{n}_{j-1s}-\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\big(\frac{{\omega}}{{\omega}+U}-\beta^{\prime}(\hat{n}_{j-1s}+\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})-\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1s}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}\big)\\
&+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{js}~\delta^{\prime}(\hat{n}_{j-1s}-\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\big(\frac{{\omega}}{{\omega}-U}-\gamma^{\prime}(\hat{n}_{j-1s}+\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})-\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1s}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}\big)~\bigg\}\end{split}

(10.17)

In this
\mathcal{O}(J^{2})
order micro-motion
\hat{y}_{2}
, we find driving-induced two-site correlated processes, including doublon-holon exchange and doublon-holon density-density interactions, as well as three-site correlated processes, including next-nearest neighbour hopping, two-electron hopping, doublon formation and dissociation.
This
\hat{y}_{2}
contributes to the following
\mathcal{O}(J^{2})
term in the lowest order Floquet Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\prime(2)}
in Eq. (
6.12
), which reads

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}([\hat{y}_{2},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}]+H.c.)\\
&=\frac{4J^{2}g^{2}}{{\omega}^{3}}(\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})\sum\limits_{j=1}^{L-1}\big(\hat{c}_{j\uparrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\downarrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1\uparrow}\hat{c}_{j+1\downarrow}+\hat{c}_{j+1\uparrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1\downarrow}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\uparrow}\hat{c}_{j\downarrow}\big)\\
&+\frac{J^{2}g^{2}}{{\omega}^{3}}(\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})\sum\limits_{j=2}^{L-1}\sum\limits_{s}\\
&\bigg\{~(\hat{c}_{j-1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1s})\big(2\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}-\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}-\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}(1-2\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}})(\hat{n}_{j-1,\bar{s}}+\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}}-2\hat{n}_{j-1\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})\big)\\
&~~+2(\hat{c}_{js}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{j+1s}+\hat{c}_{j+1s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j\bar{s}}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j-1\bar{s}}\hat{c}_{js})(1-\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1s}-\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}}+2\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j-1s}\hat{n}_{j+1\bar{s}})~\bigg\}\end{split}

(10.18)

where the coefficients are given in Eq. (
6.10
). We find this
\mathcal{O}(J^{2})
Floquet Hamiltonian contains fewer terms than the micro-motion
\hat{y}_{2}
: According to our driving term
\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}
, if a term in
\hat{y}_{2}
conserves the center-of-mass position, it will commute with
\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}
, and thus it will have no impact on the Floquet Hamiltonian (
10.18
).

10.2.2
The
\mathcal{O}(g^{2})
second-lowest order

For the second-lowest order (
n=2
) driving effects, we need to solve two Sylvester equations according to Eq. (
5.15
) for the system described by Eq. (
6.1
),

\displaystyle[\hat{f}^{(2)}_{1},\hat{H}^{(0)}]-{\omega}\hat{f}^{(2)}_{1}=0

(10.19a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]+[\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2},\hat{H}^{(0)}]-2{\omega}\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2}=0.

(10.19b)

Form these, we find
\hat{f}^{(2)}_{\pm 1}=0
in this model, and thus according to Eq. (
5.16
), there is no
\mathcal{O}(g^{3})
-correction to the Floquet Hamiltonian,
\hat{H}^{\prime(3)}=0
. More generally, only the even-order
\hat{H}^{\prime(2n)}
Floquet Hamiltonians do not vanish in the driven system described by the Hamiltonian Eq. (
6.1
). The vanishing of odd orders
can be verified using the Floquet method
[
149
]
based on Gaussian elimination.

Here we solve Eq. (
10.19b
) in the
J\ll{\omega}
limit, where we expand the solution
\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2}
in orders of hopping
J
, i.e.
\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{z}_{n}
where
\hat{z}_{n}\sim J^{n}
. This decomposes the Sylvester equation (
10.19b
) in orders of
J
, e.g.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\hat{y}_{0},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]+[\hat{z}_{0},\hat{U}]-2{\omega}\hat{z}_{0}=0

(10.20a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]+[\hat{z}_{0},\hat{h}]+[\hat{z}_{1},\hat{U}]-2{\omega}\hat{z}_{1}=0

(10.20b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\hat{y}_{2},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]+[\hat{z}_{1},\hat{h}]+[\hat{z}_{2},\hat{U}]-2{\omega}\hat{z}_{2}=0

(10.20c)

and so on. Here we also used the expansion
\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{y}_{n}
where
\hat{y}_{0}
and
\hat{y}_{1}
is solved in Appendix
10.2.1
.
According to the result Eq. (
10.8
), we find
[\hat{y}_{0},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]=0
, and thus Eq. (
10.20a
) gives
\hat{z}_{0}=0
. The source term in Eq. (
10.20b
) is then given by

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]=\frac{Jg^{2}}{2{\omega}^{2}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{L}\left(\delta_{i-j,1}+\delta_{j-i,1}\right)\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}).\end{split}

(10.21)

Similar to what we did in Appendix
10.2.1
, we solve the Sylvester equation for each term in
\frac{1}{2}[\hat{y}_{1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]
and then sum them up, which gives the solution to Eq. (
10.20b
). It reads explicitly

\begin{split}\hat{z}_{1}&=\frac{Jg^{2}}{4{\omega}^{3}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{L}\left(\delta_{i-j,1}+\delta_{j-i,1}\right)\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})(1+\beta^{\prime\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})\\
&\equiv\frac{Jg^{2}}{4{\omega}^{3}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{L}\left(\delta_{i-j,1}+\delta_{j-i,1}\right)\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}(1+\beta_{2}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma_{2}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta_{2}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})\end{split}

(10.22)

where in the first line
\beta^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}
is are simply the coefficients
\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}
with
{\omega}
replaced by
2{\omega}
. Since
\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2}=\hat{z}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(J^{2})
, we thus see from Eq. (
10.22
) that
\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2}
diverges not only when
{\omega}=U
(when
\gamma^{\prime}
diverges), but also when
{\omega}=U/2
(when
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
diverges). The divergence at
{\omega}=U/2
is understood as the two-photon resonance to excite a doublon. In the second line of Eq. (
10.22
), we have evaluated the multiplication in the first line, from which the parameters
\beta_{2},\gamma_{2},\delta_{2}
are defined for future use, i.e.
\beta_{2}\equiv\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}
,
\gamma_{2}\equiv\gamma^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}
and
\delta_{2}\equiv\delta^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}\delta^{\prime\prime}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}\delta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\delta^{\prime\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}\delta^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime}\delta^{\prime\prime}
.

10.2.3
The
\mathcal{O}(g^{3})
third-lowest order

To find the third lowest order (
n=3
) driving effect in the driving systems described by Eq. (
6.1
), we need to solve 3 Sylvester equations

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}]+\frac{1}{12}[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{-1},[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]]+\frac{2}{3}[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{H}^{\prime(2)}]+[\hat{f}^{(3)}_{1},\hat{H}^{(0)}]-{\omega}\hat{f}^{(3)}_{1}

\displaystyle=0

(10.23a)

\displaystyle[\hat{f}^{(3)}_{2},\hat{H}^{(0)}]-2{\omega}\hat{f}^{(3)}_{2}

\displaystyle=0

(10.23b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]+\frac{1}{12}[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{1}]]+[\hat{f}^{(3)}_{3},\hat{H}^{(0)}]-3{\omega}\hat{f}^{(3)}_{3}

\displaystyle=0

(10.23c)

whose solutions determine the
\mathcal{O}(g^{4})
order Floquet Hamiltonian correction,

\hat{H}^{\prime(4)}=\left(\frac{1}{2}[\hat{f}^{(3)}_{1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}]+\frac{1}{12}[\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2},[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{-1},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}]]+\frac{1}{12}[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{-1},[\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2},\hat{H}^{(1)}_{-1}]]-\frac{1}{12}[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{1},[\hat{f}^{(1)}_{-1},\hat{H}^{\prime(2)}]]\right)+H.c.

(10.24)

These higher-order solutions can be derived in the same way. We find
\hat{f}^{(3)}_{1}
diverges at
{\omega}=U,U/2
,
\hat{f}^{(3)}_{2}=0
, and
\hat{f}^{(3)}_{3}
diverges at
{\omega}=U,U/2,U/3
. This means
\hat{H}^{\prime(4)}
diverges at
{\omega}=U,U/2
.
Explicitly, by solving Eq. (
10.23a
), we find

\begin{split}\hat{f}^{(3)}_{1}&=\frac{Jg^{3}}{{\omega}^{4}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{L}\left(\delta_{i-j,1}-\delta_{j-i,1}\right)\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}(-\frac{11}{24}+\beta_{3}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma_{3}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta_{3}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})~~+\mathcal{O}(J^{2})\end{split}

(10.25)

where the parameters
\beta_{3},\gamma_{3},\delta_{3}
are determined from

\begin{split}&(-\frac{11}{24}+\beta_{3}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma_{3}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta_{3}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})\\
&~~~~=\frac{1}{8}(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})^{2}~(-1+\beta^{\prime\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})\\
&~~~~~~-\frac{1}{3}(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}})~(1+\beta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma^{\prime}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta^{\prime}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}).\end{split}

(10.26)

Inserting the solution of
\hat{f}^{(2)}_{2}
and
\hat{f}^{(3)}_{1}
, i.e. Eqs. (
10.22
) and (
10.25
), into Eq. (
10.24
), we find

\begin{split}\hat{H}^{\prime(4)}&=\frac{g^{4}J}{2{\omega}^{4}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{L}\left(\delta_{i-j,1}+\delta_{j-i,1}\right)(\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{i,s}+\hat{c}_{i,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j,s})\big(-\frac{1}{4}+\beta_{4}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\gamma_{4}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}+\delta_{4}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{i,\bar{s}}\big)\\
&=\frac{g^{4}J}{{\omega}^{4}}\sum\limits_{s}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{L-1}(\hat{c}_{j,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j+1,s}+\hat{c}_{j+1,s}^{{\dagger}}\hat{c}_{j,s})\left(-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\beta_{4}+\gamma_{4}}{2}(\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}+\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}})+\delta_{4}\hat{n}_{j,\bar{s}}\hat{n}_{j+1,\bar{s}}\right)~~+\mathcal{O}(J^{2})\end{split}

(10.27)

where we have defined
\beta_{4}\equiv\beta_{3}+\beta_{2}/24+\beta^{\prime}/6
,
\gamma_{4}\equiv\gamma_{3}+\gamma_{2}/24+\gamma^{\prime}/6
and
\delta_{4}\equiv\delta_{3}+\delta_{2}/24+\delta^{\prime}/6
. In the non-interacting case
U=0
, these 3 parameters vanish, and then
\hat{H}^{\prime(4)}
reduces exactly to the non-interacting dynamical localisation effect predicted before in Ref.
[
189
]
: The bandwidth renormalisation factor
\frac{1}{4}g^{4}/{\omega}^{4}
in Eq. (
10.27
) matches the Taylor expansion of the Bessel function
J_{0}(2g/{\omega})=1-g^{2}/{\omega}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}g^{4}/{\omega}^{4}+\mathcal{O}(g^{6})
.

10.3
Simulations on the driven Hubbard model

10.3.1
DMRG simulation of the metal-insulator phase transition

Here, we present details of the iDMRG simulation in Fig.
6.1
.
Unlike the Bose-Hubbard model
[
268
]
, the one-dimensional Fermi-Hubbard model shows a metal-insulator transition at
U/J=0
at half-filling
[
269
,
191
]
.
This makes it difficult to witness the phase transition by modifying the ratio
U/J
and calculating the single-particle gap. Therefore, we instead include a non-zero chemical potential
\mu
in Eq. (
6.2
),
and witness the (filling-control) phase transition by decreasing the chemical potential
\mu
, during which the occupancy changes from
\langle\hat{n}_{j}\rangle=1
to incommensurate values
[
191
]
.
We fix the parameters to
U=4J,{\omega}=12J,g=3J
, which is within the applicability range of our FSWT result Eq. (
6.12
).
We use infinite-DMRG to simulate the Floquet Hamiltonian’s ground state, truncating the bond dimension to
\chi=600
. The exploration around the transition shows a shift in non-commensurate occupation values and critical behaviour due to the additional correlated hopping terms compared to the HFE. To obtain the change in the critical point of the transition, we interpolated each point (in the FSWT curve) near the transition with a function
1-A\sqrt{\mu_{c}-\mu}
, typical of the commensurate-incommensurate phase transition of the Hubbard model
[
191
]
. The interpolation of HFE and the undriven curve is based on the exact Lieb-Wu equation
[
191
]
.

To confirm the applicability of our FSWT Hamiltonian (
6.12
) for Fig.
6.1
, we show in Fig.
10.1
the return rate defined in Eq. (
6.13
) on a finite
L=6
lattice, using the same parameters chosen in Fig.
6.1
. We see that the FSWT result Eq. (
6.12
), which includes the
\mathcal{O}(J)
and
\mathcal{O}(J^{2})
correlated hopping terms, matches very well with the exact dynamics throughout the simulation time, while the HFE result obviously deviates from the exact dynamics at early simulation time.
We find that the
\mathcal{O}(J^{2})
term, Eq. (
10.18
), is vital for the FSWT Hamiltonian (
6.12
) to describe the ground state and evolution accurately.

Figure 10.1
:
The return rate, Eq. (
6.13
) of a
L=6
lattice for the parameters in Fig.
6.1
, i.e.,
{\omega}=12J
,
U=4J
and
g=3J
.

10.3.2
The convergence check of TEBD over Trotter steps

In the simulation of return rates in Fig.
6.2
, the exact time-dependent Hamiltonian evolution is simulated using order-4 TEBD in TeNPy with a Trotterisation step of
dt=10^{-3}J^{-1}
